(1-O(\varepsilon))p(OPT_{skew}(\mathcal{C}_{t})\cup OPT_{small}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t}))
.

Handling small items

So far we ignored the small items in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t}):=OPT_{small}(\mathcal{C}_{t})\setminus OPT_{small}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t})
.
This set consists of small items that in the original packing intersect
a line segment of the corridor partition, the boundary of a box, or
a boundary curve within a corridor. We describe now how to add them
into the empty space of the so far computed packing. First, we assign
each item in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t})
to a grid cell. We assign each small
item
i\in OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t})
to the cell
C
such that in the original
packing
i
intersects with
C
and the area of
i\cap C
is not
smaller than
i\cap C^{\prime}
for any cell
C^{\prime}
(
i\cap C^{\prime}
denotes the
part of
i
intersecting
C^{\prime}
in the original packing for any grid
cell
C^{\prime}
).

Consider a grid cell
C
and let
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(C)
denote the small
items in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{t})
assigned to
C
. Intuitively, we want
to pack them into the empty space in the cell
C
that is not used
by any of the containers, similarly as above. We first prove an analog
of Lemma
58
of the setting above.

Lemma 63
.

Let
C
be a cell.
The total area of
C
occupied by containers is at most
(1-2\varepsilon)a(C)
.

Proof.

In our construction of the boxes we moved some of the items (within
a corridor). In particular, it can happen that we moved some items
into
C
that were originally in some other grid cell
C^{\prime}
. This
reduces the empty space in
C
for the items in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(C)
. Assume
that there is a horizontal subcorridor
H
intersecting
C
such
that some items or parts of items within
H
were moved into
C

that were not in
C
before. Then such items were moved vertically
and the corridor containing
H
must intersect the upper or lower
boundary of
C
. The part of this subcorridor lying within

C
has a height of at most
\varepsilon_{large}\cdot h(C)
. Thus, the total area
of
C
lost in this way is bounded by
O(\varepsilon_{large}a(C))
which includes
analogous vertical subcorridors.

Like in Lemma
58
we argue that in each horizontal
box of size
a\times b
we remove a horizontal strip of height
3\varepsilon b

and then the created containers lie in a box of height
(1-3\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon_{ra})b
.
In particular, if the box does not intersect the top or bottom edge
of
C
then within
C
its containers use only a box of dimension

a^{\prime}\times(1-3\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon_{ra})b
where
a^{\prime}
denotes the width of the
box within
C
, i.e., the width of the intersection of the box with

C
. If the box intersects the top or bottom edge of
C
then we
cannot guarantee that the free space lies within
C
. However, the
total area of such boxes is bounded by
O(\varepsilon_{large}a(C))
. We can apply
a symmetric argument to vertical boxes. Then, the total area of
C

used by containers is at most
(1-3\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon_{ra})a(C)+O(\varepsilon_{large}a(C))\leq(1-2\varepsilon)a(C)
.
This gives the claim of the lemma. ∎

Next, we argue that the items in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(C)
have very small total
area. Recall that they are the items intersecting
C
that are not
contained in a box. The total number of boxes and boundary curves
intersecting
C
is
O_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{large}}(1)
and in particular, this
quantity does not depend on
\varepsilon_{small}
. Hence, by choosing
\varepsilon_{small}

sufficiently small, we can ensure that
a(OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(C))\leq\varepsilon a(C)
.
Then, similarly as in Lemma
59
we can argue that
if
\varepsilon_{small}
is small enough then we can pack the items in
OPT_{small}^{\prime\prime}(C)

using NFDH into the empty space within
C
.

L&C packings

We iterate the above construction, obtaining
pairwise disjoint sets
K(1),K(2),...
until we find a set
K(k)

such that
p(K(k))\leq\varepsilon\cdot OPT
. Since the sets
K(0),K(1),...

are pairwise disjoint there must be such a value
k
with
k\leq 1/\varepsilon
.
Thus,
|\mathcal{K}(k-1)|\leq O_{\varepsilon}(1)
. We build the grid given by the

x
- and
y
-coordinates of
\mathcal{K}(k-1)
,
giving a set of cells
\mathcal{C}_{k}
. As described above we define the corridor
partition, the partition of the corridors into boxes (with the different
orders to process the subcorridors as described in Section
4.3.3
)
and finally into containers. Denote by
OPT_{small}(\mathcal{C}_{k})

the resulting set of small items.

We consider the candidate packings based on the grid
\mathcal{C}_{k}
.
For each of the six candidate packings with a degenerate
L
we can
pack almost all small items of the original packing. We define
I_{\mathrm{lc}}

and
I_{\mathrm{sc}}
the sets of items in long and short subcorridors in the initial
corridor partition, respectively. Exactly as in the cardinality case,
a subcorridor is long if it is longer than
N/2
and short otherwise.
As before we divide the items into fat and thin items and define the
sets
OPT_{SF}
,
OPT_{LT}
, and
OPT_{ST}
accordingly. Moreover, we define the
set
OPT_{LF}
to contain all items in
I_{\mathrm{lc}}
that are fat in all candidate
packings
plus
the items in
\mathcal{K}(k-1)
.

Thus, we obtain the respective claims of Lemmas
54
,

55
, and
56
in the weighted setting.
For the following lemma let
OPT_{small}:=OPT_{small}(\mathcal{C}_{k})
.

Lemma 64
.

Let
OPT_{L\&C}
the most profitable
solution that is packed by an L&C packing.

(a)

p(OPT_{L\&C})\geq(1-O(\varepsilon))(p(OPT_{LF})+p(OPT_{SF})+p(OPT_{small}))

(b)

p(OPT_{L\&C})\geq(1-O(\varepsilon))(p(OPT_{LF})+p(OPT_{SF})/2+p(OPT_{LT})/2+p(OPT_{small}))

(c)

p(OPT_{L\&C})\geq(1-O(\varepsilon))(p(OPT_{LF})+p(OPT_{SF})/2+p(OPT_{ST})/2+p(OPT_{small})).

For the candidate packing for the non-degenerate-
L
case
(Lemma
57
in Section
4.3.5
) we first add the small items as described
above. Then we remove the items in
\mathcal{K}(k-1)
.
Then, like above, with a random shift we delete items touching a horizontal
and a vertical strip of width
3\varepsilon N
. Like before, each item
i

is still contained in the resulting solution with probability
1/2-15\varepsilon

(note that we cannot make such a claim for the items in
\mathcal{K}(k-1)
).
For each small item we can even argue that it still contained in the
resulting solution with probability
1-O(\varepsilon)
(since it is that
small in both dimensions).
We proceed with the construction of the
boundary
L
and the assignment of the items into it like in the
unweighted case.

Lemma 65
.

For the solution

OPT_{L\&C}
we have that
p(OPT_{L\&C})\geq(1-O(\varepsilon))(\frac{3}{4}p(OPT_{LT})+p(OPT_{ST})+\frac{1-O(\varepsilon)}{2}p(OPT_{SF})+p(OPT_{small}))
.

When we combine Lemmas
64
and
65

we conclude that
p(OPT_{L\&C})\geq(17/9+O(\varepsilon))p(OPT)
. Similarly
as before, the worst case is obtained, up to
1-O(\varepsilon)
factors,
when we have that
p(OPT_{LT})=p(OPT_{SF})=p(OPT_{ST})
,
p(OPT_{LF})=5p(OPT_{LT})/4
, and
p(OPT_{small})=0
. This completes the proof of Lemma
52
.

4.3.8
Main algorithm

In this Section we present our main algorithm for the weighted case
of 2DGK. It is in fact an approximation scheme for L&C packings.
Its approximation factor therefore follows immediately from Lemma
52
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, we first guess the quantities
\varepsilon_{large},\varepsilon_{small}
,
the proof of Lemma
47
reveals that there
are only
2/\epsilon+1
values we need to consider. We choose
\varepsilon_{ring}:=\epsilon^{2}

and subsequently define
\varepsilon_{box}
according to Lemma
50
.
Our algorithm combines two basic packing procedures. The first one
is PTAS to pack items into a constant number
of containers given by Theorem
12
.

The second packing procedure is the PTAS for the L-packing problem, see Theorem
34
.

To use these packing procedures, we first guess whether the optimal
L&C-packing due to Lemma
52
uses a non-degenerate
boundary L.
If yes, we guess a parameter
\ell
which denotes the
minimum height of the vertical items in the boundary
L
and the
minimum width of the horizontal items in the boundary
L
. For
\ell

we allow all heights and widths of the input items that are larger than

N/2
, i.e., at most
2n
values. Let
I_{\mathrm{long}}
be the items whose
longer side has length at least
\ell
(hence longer than
N/2
).
We set the width of the boundary
L
to be
\epsilon^{2}N
and solve
the resulting instance
(L,I_{\mathrm{long}})
optimally using the PTAS for L-packings
due to Theorem
34
. Then we enumerate all the possible subsets of
non-overlapping containers in the space not occupied by the boundary

L
(or in the full knapsack, in the case of a degenerate
L
),
where the number and sizes of the containers are defined as in Lemma

51
. In particular, there are at most
O_{\varepsilon}(1)

containers and there is a set of size
n^{O_{\varepsilon}(1)}
that
we can compute in polynomial time such that the height and the width
of each container is contained in this set. We compute an approximate
solution for the resulting container packing instance with items
I_{\mathrm{short}}=I\setminus I_{\mathrm{long}}

using the PTAS from Theorem
12
. Finally,
we output the most profitable solution that we computed.

4.4
Cardinality case without rotations

In this section, we present a refined algorithm with approximation factor of
\frac{558}{325}+\varepsilon<1.717
for the cardinality case when rotations are not allowed.

Theorem 66
.

There exists a polynomial-time
\frac{558}{325}+\varepsilon<1.717
-approximation algorithm for cardinality 2DK.

Along this section, we will use
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{ring},\varepsilon_{large},\varepsilon_{small}
as defined in Section
4.3
.
Let us define
\varepsilon_{L}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Note than
\varepsilon_{L}\geq\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon_{large}\geq\varepsilon_{small}
.
For simplicity and readability of the section, sometimes we will slightly abuse the notation and for any small constant depending on
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{ring},\varepsilon_{large},\varepsilon_{small}
, we will just use
O({\varepsilon_{L}})
. Also, since the profit of each item is equal to
1
, instead of
p(I)
for a set of items
I
we will just write
|I|
.
We will again consider the
L\&C
packing as in Section
4.3
and use
I_{\mathrm{long}},I_{\mathrm{short}},

OPT_{LF},OPT_{LT},

OPT_{SF},OPT_{ST}
etc. as defined there.
We will assume as in the proof of Lemma
57
that
\ell=\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
. That way we make sure that no long rectangle belongs to a short subcorridor.
Let us define
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}:=I_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT
and
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}:=I_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT
.
Now we give a brief informal overview of the refinement and the cases before we go to the details.

Overview of refined packing.

For the refined packing we will consider several
L\&C
packings.
Some of the packings are just extensions of previous packings (such as from Theorem
46
and Lemma
64
).
Then we consider several other new
L\&C
packings where an
L
-region is packed with items from
I_{\mathrm{long}}
and the remaining region is used for packing items from
I_{\mathrm{short}}
using Steinberg’s theorem (See Theorem
3
).
Note that in the definition of
L\&C
packing in Section
4.3
, we assumed the height of the horizontal part of
L
-region and width of the vertical part of
L
-region are the same. However, for these new packings we will consider
L
-region where the height of the horizontal part and width of vertical part may not be the same.
Now several cases arise depending on the structure and profit of the
L
-region. To pack items in
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
we have three options:

1. We can pack items in
I_{\mathrm{short}}
using Steinberg’s theorem into one rectangular region. Then we need both sides of the region to be greater than
\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}
.

2. We can pack items in
I_{\mathrm{short}}
using Steinberg’s theorem such that
vertical and horizontal items are packed separately into different vertical and horizontal rectangular regions inside the knapsack.

3. If
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})
is large, we might pack only a small region with items in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
, and use the remaining larger space in the knapsack to pack significant fraction of profits from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
.

Now depending on the structure of
L
-packing and
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})
, we arrive at several different cases.
If the
L
-region has very small width and height, we have case (1).
Else if
L
-region has very large width (or height), we have case (2B), where we pack nearly

\frac{1}{2}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|
in the
L
-region and then pack items from
I_{\mathrm{short}}
in one large rectangular region.
Otherwise, we have case (2A), where either we pack only items from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}
(See Lemma
69
, used in case: (2Ai)) or nearly
3/4|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|
(See Lemma
70
, used in cases (2Aii), (2Aiiia)) or in another case, we pack the vertical and horizontal items in
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
in two different regions through a more complicated packing (See case (2Aiiib)). The details of these cases will be clearer in the proof of Theorem
66
.

Now first, we start with some extensions of previous packings.
Note that by using analogous arguments as in the proof of Theorem
46
, we can derive the following inequalities leading to a
\left(\frac{16}{9}+O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)
-approximation algorithm.

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|

(4.1)

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+\left(\frac{3}{4}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|

(4.2)

Now from Lemma
50
, items in
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
can be packed into two containers of size
\ell\times\varepsilon_{ring}N
and
\varepsilon_{ring}N\times\ell
. We can adapt part of the results in Lemma
64
to obtain the following inequalities.

Proposition 67
.

The following inequalities hold:

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq(1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\setminus OPT_{T}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|).

(4.3)

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq(1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\setminus OPT_{T}|+\frac{1}{2}(|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}|)).

(4.4)

Proof.

Inequality (
4.3
) follows directly from Lemma
64
since
OPT_{LF}\cup OPT_{SF}\cup OPT_{small}=(OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\setminus OPT_{T})\cup(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})
and both groups of sets are disjoint. Inequality (
4.4
) follows from Lemma
55
: if we consider the sum of the number of packed rectangles corresponding to the
4
subcases associated with the case “short horizontal/short vertical”, then every
i\in OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\setminus OPT_{T}\subseteq OPT_{LF}
appears four times, every
i\in OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{LF}
appears four times, every
i\in OPT_{SF}
appears twice and every
i\in OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}
appears twice. After including a
(1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))
fraction of
OPT_{small}
, and since
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{LF})\cup OPT_{SF}\cup OPT_{small}=OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
, the inequality follows by taking average of the four packings ∎

The following lemma is a consequence of Steinberg’s Theorem
3
.

Lemma 68
.

Let
I^{\prime}
be a set of items such that
\displaystyle\max_{i\in I^{\prime}}0pt(i)\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
and
\displaystyle\max_{i\in I^{\prime}}0pt(i)\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
.
Then for any
\alpha,\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}
, all of
I^{\prime}
can be packed into a knapsack of width
(1-\alpha)N
and height
(1-\beta)N
if

a(I^{\prime})\leq\Big(\frac{1}{2}-(\alpha+\beta)\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)-8\varepsilon_{large}^{2}\Big)N^{2}.

Proof.

From Steinberg’s Theorem,
I^{\prime}
can be packed in a rectangle of size
(1-\alpha)N\times(1-\beta)N

if

\displaystyle 2a(I^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha)(1-\beta)N^{2}

\displaystyle-\left(2\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N-(1-\alpha)N\right)_{+}\left(2\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N-(1-\beta)N\right)_{+}

\displaystyle=\Big(1-(\alpha+\beta)(1-4\varepsilon_{large})-16\varepsilon_{large}^{2}\Big)N^{2}.

∎

Now we prove a more general version of Lemma
57
which holds for the cardinality case.

Lemma 69
.

If
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq\gamma N^{2}
for any
\gamma\leq 1
, then

\displaystyle|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\,\,

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|

\displaystyle+\min\left\{1,\frac{1-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{2\gamma}\right\}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|.

Proof.

As in Lemma
57
, we can pack
\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}|
and
|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|
in a boundary
L
-region plus two boxes on the other two sides of the knapsack
and then a free square region with side length
(1-3\varepsilon)N
can be used to pack items from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
.
From Lemma
68
, any subset of rectangles of
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
with total area at most
(1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))N^{2}/2
can be packed into that square region of length
(1-3\varepsilon)N
.
Thus we sort rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
in the order of nondecreasing area and iteratively pick them until their total area reaches
(1-O(\varepsilon_{L})-\varepsilon_{small})N^{2}/2
. Using Steinberg’s theorem, there exists a packing of the selected rectangles. If
2\gamma\leq 1-O(\varepsilon_{L})-\varepsilon_{small}
then the profit of this packing is
|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
, and otherwise the total profit is at least
\frac{1-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{2\gamma}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
. The packing coming from Steinberg’s theorem may not be container-based, but we can then use resource augmentation as in Lemma
57
to obtain an
L\&C
packing.
∎

Now the following lemma will be useful when
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})
is large.

Lemma 70
.

If
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})>\gamma N^{2}
for any
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{large}
, then

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+\frac{(3\gamma-1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{4\gamma}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|.

Proof.

Similar to Lemma
45
in Section
4.2
, we start from the optimal packing and move all rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
to the boundary such that all of them are contained in a boundary ring. Note that unlike the case when we only pack
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}
in the boundary region, the boundary ring formed by
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
may have width or height
>>\varepsilon_{ring}N
.
Let us call the 4 stacks in the ring to be subrings.
Let us assume that left and right subrings have width
\alpha_{1}N
and
\alpha_{2}N
respectively and
bottom and top subrings have height
\beta_{1}N
and
\beta_{2}N
respectively.
It is easy to see that we can arrange the subrings such that
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\leq 1/2
.

As
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})>\gamma N^{2}
, then
a(OPT_{\mathrm{long}})<(1-\gamma)N^{2}
.
Let us define
\alpha=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
and
\beta=\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}
.
Then
(\alpha+\beta)N\cdot\frac{N}{2}\leq a(OPT_{\mathrm{long}})
. Hence,
\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}<1-\gamma
.
Thus we get the following two inequalities:

(\alpha+\beta)\leq 2(1-\gamma)

(4.5)

a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}})\leq\left(1-\frac{(\alpha+\beta)}{2}\right)N^{2}

(4.6)

Now consider the case when we remove the top horizontal subring and construct a boundary L-region as in Lemma
45
. We will assume that rectangles in the
L
-region are pushed to the left and bottom as much as possible. Then, the boundary
L
-region has width
(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})N
and height
\beta_{1}N
. We will use Steinberg’s theorem to show the existence of a packing of rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
in a subregion of the remaining space with width
N-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\varepsilon)N
and height
N-(\beta_{1}+\varepsilon)N
, and use the rest of the area for resource augmentation to get an
L\&C
-based packing.
Since
\gamma\geq\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{large}
, we have that
\alpha+\beta+2\varepsilon\leq 1/2-2\varepsilon_{large}
.
So
\alpha+\varepsilon\leq 1/2-2\varepsilon_{large}
and
\beta+\varepsilon\leq 1/2-2\varepsilon_{large}
.
Thus from Lemma
68
, in the region with width
N-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\varepsilon)N
and height
N(1-\beta_{1}-\varepsilon)
we can pack rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
of total area at most
\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1})}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\Big)N^{2}
.
Hence, we can take the rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
in the order of nondecreasing area until their total area reaches

\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1})}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L})-\varepsilon_{small}\Big)N^{2}
and pack at least
|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|\cdot\frac{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1})}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L})-\varepsilon_{small})}{(1-\frac{(\alpha+\beta)}{2})}
using Steinberg’s theorem.
If we now consider all the four different cases corresponding to removal of the four different subrings and take the average of profits obtained in each case,
we pack at least

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|\cdot\left(\frac{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{8}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2})-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{(1-\frac{(\alpha+\beta)}{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|\cdot\left(\frac{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{8}(\alpha+\beta)-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{(1-\frac{(\alpha+\beta)}{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|\cdot\frac{3\gamma-1-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{4\gamma},

where the last inequality follows from (
4.5
) and the fact that the expression is decreasing as a function of
(\alpha+\beta)
.∎

Now we start with the proof of Theorem
66
.

Proof of Theorem
66
.

In the refined analysis, we will consider different solutions and show that the best of them always achieves the claimed approximation guarantee. We will pack some rectangles in a boundary
L
-region (either a subset of only
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}
or a subset of
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
) using the PTAS algorithm for boundary
L
-region described in Section
4.1
, and in the remaining area of the knapsack (outside of the boundary
L
-region), we will pack a subset of rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}
.

Figure 4.7
:
Summary of the cases.

Consider the ring as constructed in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
70
.
Then we remove the least profitable subring and repack the remaining rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
in a boundary
L
-region.
Without loss of generality, assume that the horizontal top subring was the least profitable subring. The other cases are analogous.
We will use the same notation as in Lemma
70
, and also define
w_{L}=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}),h_{L}=\beta_{1}
.
Now let us consider two cases (see Figure
4.7
for the overview of the subcases of case 2).

\bullet
Case 1.
w_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L},h_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L}
.

In this case, following the proof of Lemma
69
(using
\gamma=1
), we can pack
\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|+\frac{1-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{2}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
.
This along with inequalities (
4.2
), (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) gives

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{5}{8}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.7)

\bullet
Case 2.
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
or
h_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
.

Let
V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}}
be the set of vertical rectangles having height strictly larger than
(1-2\varepsilon_{L})N
. Let us define
w(V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})=\sum_{i\in V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}}}0pt(i)
.
Similarly, let
H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}}
be the set of vertical rectangles of width strictly larger than
(1-2\varepsilon_{L})N
and
h(H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})=\sum_{i\in H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}}}0pt(i)
.

\Diamond
Case 2A.
(
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
and
h_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
)
or
(
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L},h_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L},
and
w(V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
)
or
(
h_{L}>\varepsilon_{L},w_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L},
and
h(H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
)
.

We show that if any one of the above three conditions is met, then we can pack
\frac{3(1-O(\varepsilon))}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|
in a boundary
L
-region of width at most
(w_{L}+\varepsilon_{L})N
and height at most
(h_{L}+\varepsilon_{L})N
, and
then in the remaining area we will pack some rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
using Steinberg’s theorem and resource augmentation.

(a)

Packing of
L
-region using rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
. Striped strips are cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width and cheapest
\varepsilon N
-height.

(b)

Packing of rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cup(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T})
. Dark gray rectangles are from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
.

Figure 4.8
:
The case for
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
and
h_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
.

(a)

Packing of
L
-region using rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
. Striped strip is the cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width strip.

(b)

Packing of rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cup(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T})
. Dark gray rectangles are from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
.

Figure 4.9
:
The case for
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
and
h_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L}
.

Packing of subset of rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cup(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T})
into
L
-region.

If (
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
and
h_{L}>\varepsilon_{L}
), we partition the vertical part of the
L
-region into consecutive strips of width
\varepsilon N
.
Consider the strip that intersects the least number of vertical rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
among all strips, and we call it to be the
cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width vertical strip
(See Figure
4.8(a)
).
Clearly the cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width vertical strip intersects at most a
\frac{\varepsilon+2\varepsilon_{small}}{\varepsilon_{L}}\leq O(\varepsilon_{L})
fraction of the rectangles in the vertical part of
L
-region, so we can remove all such vertical rectangles intersected by the cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width vertical strip at a small loss of profit.
Similarly, we remove the horizontal rectangles intersected by the cheapest
\varepsilon N
-height horizontal strip in the boundary
L
-region and push the remaining rectangles in the
L
region to the bottom and left.
We now pack the horizontal container for
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
above the horizontal part of
L
region and the vertical container for
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
to the right of the vertical part of the
L
-region (See Figure
4.8(b)
).

In the other case (
w_{L}>\varepsilon_{L},h_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L}
and
w(V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
), we can again remove the cheapest
\varepsilon N
-width vertical strip in the boundary
L
-region and pack the vertical container for
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
there (See Figure
4.9(a)
). Now we show how to pack horizontal items from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
.
In the packing of the boundary
L
-region, we can assume that the vertical rectangles are sorted non-increasingly by height from left to right and pushed upwards until they touch the top boundary.
Then, since
w(V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
and (
h_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L}
), the region
\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N,N\right]\times\left[\varepsilon_{L}N,2\varepsilon_{L}N\right]
will be completely empty and thus we will have enough space to pack the horizontal container for
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
on top of the horizontal part of the
L
-region (See Figure
4.9(b)
).
The last case, when
w_{L}\leq\varepsilon_{L}
, is analogous.
Thus we have a packing in boundary
L
-region of width at most
(w_{L}+\varepsilon_{L})N
and height at most
(h_{L}+\varepsilon_{L})N
with total profit at least
\frac{3(1-O(\varepsilon))}{4}(|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|)+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|
, implying that

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\frac{3(1-O(\varepsilon))}{4}(|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|)+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|

(4.8)

Packing of subset of rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
into the remaining region.

Note that after packing rectangles of cardinality at least
\frac{3(1-O(\varepsilon))}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|
in the boundary
L
-region, the remaining rectangular region, let us call it to be
\mathcal{R}_{container}
, of width
(1-w_{L}-\varepsilon_{L})N
and height
(1-h_{L}-\varepsilon_{L})N
is completely empty.
Now we will show the existence of a packing of some rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
in the remaining space of the packing (even using some space from the
L
-boundary region).
Let
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}:=(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\cap R_{hor}
and
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}:=(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\cap R_{ver}
.
Let us assume without loss of generality that vertical rectangles are shifted as much as possible to the left and top of the knapsack and horizontal are pushed as much as possible to the right and bottom. We divide the analysis in three subcases depending on
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})
.

-
Subcase (i).
If
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq\frac{3}{5}N^{2}
, from inequalities (
4.2
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
), (
4.8
) and Lemma
69
we get

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{127}{216}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.9)

-
Subcase (ii).
If
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})>(\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{large})N^{2}
, from inequalities (
4.2
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
), (
4.8
) and Lemma
70
we get

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{17}{28}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.10)

-
Subcase (iii).
Finally, if
\frac{3}{5}N^{2}\leq a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq(\frac{3}{4}+\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{large})N^{2}
, from inequality (
4.5
) we get,
\alpha+\beta\leq 2(1-\frac{3}{5})\leq\frac{4}{5}
. Now we consider two subcases.

\odot
Subcase (iii a):
w_{L}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
h_{L}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

Note that in this case if
w_{L}\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}-2\varepsilon_{L}
or
h_{L}\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}-2\varepsilon_{L}
,
we can remove the cheapest
2(\varepsilon_{L}+\varepsilon_{large})N
-width vertical strip and the cheapest
2(\varepsilon_{L}+\varepsilon_{large})N
-height horizontal strip from the
L
-region.
Otherwise we have
w_{L}<\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}-2\varepsilon_{L}
and
h_{L}<\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}-2\varepsilon_{L}
.
So there is free rectangular region that has both side lengths at least
N(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}+\varepsilon_{L})
; we will keep
\varepsilon_{L}N
width and
\varepsilon_{L}N
height for resource augmentation and use the rest of the rectangular region (with both sides length at least
\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
) for showing existence of a packing using Steinberg’s theorem.
Note that this rectangular region has area at least
N^{2}(1-w_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})(1-h_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})
.
Thus by using Steinberg’s theorem, we can pack either rectangles from
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}
(by sorting them non-decreasingly by area and picking them iteratively) having total area at least
min\{a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}),\frac{N^{2}(1-w_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})(1-h_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})}{2}-\varepsilon_{small}\}
or rectangles from
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}
with total area at least
min\{a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}),\frac{N^{2}(1-w_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})(1-h_{L}-2\varepsilon_{L})}{2}-\varepsilon_{small}\}
.
We claim that if we keep the best of the two packings, we can always pack at least
\left(\frac{7}{48}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
.
Note that it is sufficient to consider,
\frac{N^{2}(1-w_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(1-h_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{2}<\min\{a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}),a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver})\}
, as otherwise, we can pack even more rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
than the claimed fraction.
In this case, we pack at least

\frac{N^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{(1-w_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(1-h_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{2a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor})}|(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}|+\frac{(1-w_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(1-h_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{2a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver})}|(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}|\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\frac{N^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{(1-w_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(1-h_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{2a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|\right)

where the inequality follows from the fact that
\frac{a}{b}+\frac{c}{d}\geq\frac{(a+c)}{(b+d)}
for
a,b,c,d\geq 0
.
Since
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{\beta}{2})N^{2}\leq(1-\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\frac{h_{L}}{2})N^{2}

and
w_{L}+h_{L}\leq\alpha+\beta\leq\frac{4}{5}
, we can minimize the expression

\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{N^{2}(1-w_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))(1-h_{L}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))}{2a(SF)}|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|\right)

over the domain
\{w_{L}+h_{L}\leq\frac{4}{5},0\leq w_{L}\leq\frac{1}{2},0\leq h_{L}\leq\frac{1}{2}\}
, obtaining that this is at least
(\frac{7}{48}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
(value reached at
w_{L}=\frac{1}{2}
and
h_{L}=\frac{3}{10}
).
This together with inequalities (
4.2
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
), (
4.8
) and Lemma
69
implies that

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{215}{369}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.11)

Figure 4.10
:
Case 2A(iii)b in the proof of Theorem
66

\odot
Subcase (iii b):

w_{L}>\frac{1}{2}
(then from inequality (
4.5
),
h_{L}\leq\frac{3}{10}
).
Note that
a(OPT_{\mathrm{long}})\leq(1-\frac{3}{5})N^{2}=\frac{2}{5}N^{2}
.

Let us define some parameters from the current packing to simplify the calculations. Let
\lambda N
be the height of the tallest vertical rectangle in the packing that touches the vertical line
x=\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
and
\gamma N
be the total width of vertical rectangles having height greater than
(1-h_{L})N
. We define also the following three regions in the knapsack:
\mathcal{A}
, the rectangular region of width
w_{L}N
and height
\frac{1}{2}N
in the top left corner of the knapsack;
\mathcal{B}
, the rectangular region of width
w_{L}N
and height
(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})N
below
\mathcal{A}
and left-aligned with the knapsack; and
\mathcal{C}
, the rectangular region of width
N
and height
h_{L}N
touching the bottom boundary of the knapsack. Notice that
\mathcal{A}
is fully occupied by vertical rectangles,
\mathcal{B}
is almost fully occupied by vertical rectangles except for the right region of width
w_{L}N-\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
, and at least half of
\mathcal{C}
is occupied by horizontal rectangles (some vertical rectangles may overlap with this region).
Our goal is to pack some rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
in the

L

-shaped region outside
\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{C}
. Let
\psi\in[\lambda,1-h_{L}]
be a parameter to be fixed. We will use, when possible, the following regions for packing items from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
: Region 1 on the top right corner of the knapsack with width
N(1-w_{L})
and height
\psi N
and Region 2 which is the rectangular region
[0,N]\times[h_{L}N,(1-\psi)\cdot N]
(see Figure
4.10
). Region
1
is completely free but Region 2 may overlap with vertical rectangles.

We will now divide Region 2 into a constant number of boxes such that: they do not overlap with vertical rectangles, the total area inside Region 2 which is neither overlapping with vertical rectangles nor covered by boxes is at most
O(\varepsilon_{L})N^{2}
and each box has width at least
\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
and height at least
\varepsilon N
. That way we will be able to pack rectangles from
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}
into the box defined by Region
1
and rectangles from
(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}
into the boxes defined inside Region 2 using almost completely its free space.
In order to create the boxes inside Region 2 we first create a monotone chain by doing the following: Let
(x_{1},y_{1})=(\gamma N,h_{L})
. Starting from position
(x_{1},y_{1})
, we draw an horizontal line of length
\varepsilon_{L}N
and then a vertical line from bottom to top until it touches a vertical rectangle, reaching position
(x_{2},y_{2})
. From
(x_{2},y_{2})
we start again the same procedure and iterate until we reach the vertical line
x=\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
or the horizontal line
y=(1-\psi)N
.
Notice that the area above the monotone chain and below
y=(1-\psi)N
that is not occupied by vertical rectangles, is at most
\sum_{i}{\varepsilon_{L}N(y_{i+1}-y_{i})}\leq\varepsilon_{L}N^{2}
.
The number of points
(x_{i},y_{i})
defined in the previous procedure is at most
1/\varepsilon_{L}
. By drawing an horizontal line starting from each
(x_{i},y_{i})
up to
(N,y_{i})
, together with the drawn lines from the monotone chain and the right limit of the knapsack, we define
k\leq 1/\varepsilon_{L}
boxes. We discard the boxes having height less than
\varepsilon N
, whose total area is at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{L}}N^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{L}N^{2}
, and have all the desired properties for the boxes.

Since the total area of rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
is at most
\frac{2}{5}N^{2}
, the total area not occupied by boxes in Region 2 is at most
N^{2}(\frac{2}{5}-\frac{1}{2}w_{L}-(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}h_{L})=N^{2}(\frac{13}{20}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{h_{L}}{2})
, which comes from the area we know is occupied for sure in regions
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
by rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
. This implies that the total area of the horizontal boxes is at least
N^{2}(1-\psi-h_{L})-N^{2}(\frac{13}{20}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{h_{L}}{2})
and the area of the vertical box is
(1-w_{L})\psi
.
These two areas become equal if we can set
\psi=\frac{7+10(w_{L}+\lambda-h_{L})}{40-20w_{L}}
. It is not difficult to verify that in this case
\psi\leq 1-h_{L}
.
If
\frac{7+10(w_{L}+\lambda-h_{L})}{40-20w_{L}}\geq\lambda
, then we set

\psi=\frac{7+10(w_{L}+\lambda-h_{L})}{40-20w_{L}}
. Otherwise we set
\psi=\lambda
.

First, consider when
\psi=\frac{7+10(w_{L}+\lambda-h_{L})}{40-20w_{L}}
.
Since
\psi\geq\lambda
, the width of the boxes inside Region 2 is at least
\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
and the box in Region
1
has height at least
\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
. By using Steinberg’s theorem, we can always pack in these boxes at least

\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{\frac{1}{2}(N-w_{L})\psi N}{a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor})}-\varepsilon_{small}\right\}\right)|(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{hor}|+\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{\frac{1}{2}(N-w_{L})\psi N}{a((OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver})}-\varepsilon_{small}\right\}\right)|(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})_{ver}|.

Note that from each box
B^{\prime}
of height
h(\geq\varepsilon N)
, we can remove the cheapest
\varepsilon h
-horizontal strip and use resource augmentation to get a container based packing with nearly the same profit as
B^{\prime}
.
Thus by performing a similar analysis to the one done in Subcase (iii a), and using the fact that
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq N^{2}-(\frac{\alpha}{2}+(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2})N^{2}\leq N^{2}-N^{2}(\frac{w_{L}}{2}+(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})\frac{1}{2}+\frac{h_{L}}{2})
, we can minimize the whole expression over the domain
\{\frac{w_{L}}{2}+(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})\frac{1}{2}+\frac{h_{L}}{2}\leq\frac{2}{5},\frac{1}{2}\leq w_{L}\leq\frac{4}{5},\frac{1}{2}\leq\lambda\leq 1,0\leq\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}\}
and prove that this solution packs at least

\left(\frac{3-O(\varepsilon_{L})}{4}\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|+\left(\frac{5}{36}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|.

Thus, using the above inequality along with (
4.2
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and Lemma
69
, we get

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{325}{558}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.12)

Finally, if
\psi=\lambda<\frac{7+10(w_{L}+\lambda-h_{L})}{40-20w_{L}}
, we will not get equal area horizontal and vertical boxes for packing of items in
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
.
In this case we change the width of the box inside Region
1
to be
w_{L}^{\prime}<N(1-w_{L})
fixed in such a way that the area of this box is equal to the bound we have for the area of the boxes in Region
2
, i.e.,
N^{2}(1-\lambda-h_{L})-(\frac{13}{20}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\frac{h_{L}}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2}+O(\varepsilon_{L}))N^{2}
. Performing the same analysis as before, it can be shown that in this case we pack at least

\left(\frac{(1-\lambda-h_{L})N^{2}-(\frac{13}{20}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\frac{h_{L}}{2}-\frac{\lambda}{2})N^{2}}{2a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|,

which is at least
(\frac{1}{6}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
over the domain
\{\frac{w_{L}}{2}+(\lambda-\frac{1}{2})\frac{1}{2}+\frac{h_{L}}{2}\leq\frac{2}{5},\psi<\lambda,\frac{1}{2}\leq w_{L}\leq\frac{4}{5},\frac{1}{2}\leq\lambda\leq 1,0\leq\beta\leq\frac{3}{10}\}
(and this solution is then better than (
4.12
)).

(a)

Packing of
L
-region using rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}
. Striped rectangles are removed.

(b)

Packing of rectangles in
OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cup(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T})
. Dark gray rectangles are from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
.

Figure 4.11
:
The case 2B.

\Diamond
Case 2B.
(
h_{L}<\varepsilon_{L}
and
w(V_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})>\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
)
or
(
w_{L}<\varepsilon_{L}
and
h(H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})>\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
)

In the first case
a(OPT_{\mathrm{long}})>\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)(1-2\varepsilon_{L})N^{2}+(w_{L}-\frac{1}{2})N\cdot\frac{N}{2}\geq(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{w_{L}}{2}-\varepsilon_{L}-2\varepsilon_{large})N^{2}
.
Thus
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})<(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}+\varepsilon_{L}+2\varepsilon_{large})N^{2}
.

Now consider the vertical rectangles in the boundary
L
-region sorted non-increasingly by width and pick them iteratively
until their total width crosses
(\frac{w_{L}}{2}+3\varepsilon_{L}+2\varepsilon_{large})N
.
Remove these rectangles and push the remaining vertical rectangles in the
L
-region to the left as much as possible.
This modified
L
-region will have profit at least
(\frac{1}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|
.
Now we can put
\varepsilon N
-strip for the vertical items from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
next to the vertical part of
L
-region. On the other hand, horizontal part of
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}
can be placed on top of horizontal part of
L
-region.
The remaining space will be a free rectangular region of height at least
(1-2\varepsilon_{L})N
and width
(1-\frac{w_{L}}{2}+2\varepsilon_{L}+2\varepsilon_{large})N
.
We will use a part of this rectangular region of height
(1-3\varepsilon_{L})N
and width
(1-\frac{w_{L}}{2}+\varepsilon_{L})N
to pack rectangles from
OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}
and the rest of the region for resource augmentation.
Using Lemma
68
, we can pack small rectangles in this region with profit at least
\Big(\frac{(1-\frac{w_{L}}{2})/2}{\frac{3}{4}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\Big)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|\geq(\frac{3}{4}-O(\varepsilon_{L}))|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|
as
w_{L}\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, we get,

\displaystyle|OPT_{L\&C}|

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}|+|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\cap OPT_{T}|

(4.13)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{3}{4}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T}|

On the other hand, as
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq(\frac{3}{4}-\frac{w_{L}}{2}+\varepsilon_{L}+2\varepsilon_{large})N^{2}
and
w_{L}>1/2
, we get
a(OPT_{\mathrm{short}}\setminus OPT_{T})\leq\frac{1}{2}N^{2}
and thus from Lemma
69
we get,

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\frac{3}{4}|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap OPT_{T}|+(1-O(\varepsilon_{L}))|OPT_{\mathrm{short}}|

(4.14)

From inequalities (
4.1
), (
4.3
), (
4.4
),
(
4.13
), (
4.14
), we get,

|OPT_{L\&C}|\geq\left(\frac{24}{41}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|

(4.15)

Now we consider the last case when
w_{L}<\varepsilon_{L}
and
h(H_{1-2\varepsilon_{L}})>\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon_{large}\right)N
.
Note that as we assumed the cheapest subring was the top subring, after removing it we might be left with only
|OPT_{\mathrm{long}}\cap R_{hor}|/2
profit in the horizontal part of
L
-region. So, further removal of items from the horizontal part might not give us a good solution.
Thus we show an alternate good packing. We restart with the ring packing and delete the cheapest vertical subring instead of the cheapest subring (i.e., the top subring) and create a new boundary
L
-region.
Here, consider the horizontal rectangles in the boundary
L
-region in non-increasing order of height and take them
until their total height crosses
(\frac{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}{2}+\varepsilon_{small}+\varepsilon)N
. Remove these rectangles and push the remaining horizontal rectangles to the bottom as much as possible.
Then, following similar arguments as in case 2B, we will obtain the same bounds as in inequality (
4.15
).

In summary, from all the cases (see Figure
4.7
for the overview of the case analysis) the profit of the obtained solution is at least

\left(\frac{325}{558}-O(\varepsilon_{L})\right)|OPT|.

∎

4.5
Conclusions

In this chapter, we presented techniques for 2DGK that are not entirely based on containers. By leveraging a combination of container packings and L-packings, we obtained the first algorithm that break the barrier of
2
for the approximation factor of this problem. We obtained an approximation factor of
17/9+\varepsilon<1.89
for the weighted case, and
\frac{558}{325}+\varepsilon<1.72
in the cardinality case.

Further improvements could come from the study of generalizations of L-packings. For example, a PTAS for
ring-packing
instances arising by shifting of long items would lead to an improved approximation factor. A PTAS for
O(1)
simultaneous L-packings would also be an interesting development, and might be a stepping stone for the long-standing problem of finding a PTAS for 2DGK, which remains open even in the cardinality case, and even if pseudo-polynomial time is allowed instead of polynomial time.

Chapter 5
Approximations for 2DGK

with Rotations

In this chapter, we consider the 2DGKR problem, where we are allowed to rotate the rectangles by
90^{\circ}
.

The possibility to rotate the rectangles makes the problem significantly easier. In fact, we obtain better approximation factors, and we do not need L-packings anymore, obtaining purely container-based algorithms.

The basic idea is that any thin item can now be packed inside a narrow vertical strip
(say at the right edge of the knapsack) by possibly rotating it. This
way we do not lose one quarter of the profit due to the mapping to
an L-packing and instead place all items from the ring into
the mentioned strip (while we ensure that their total width is small).
The remaining short items are packed by means of a novel
resource
contraction
lemma: unless there is one
huge item
that occupies almost
the whole knapsack (a case that we consider separately), we can pack
almost one half of the profit of non-thin items in a
reduced

knapsack where one of the two sides is shortened by a factor
1-\varepsilon

(hence leaving enough space for the vertical strip). Thus, roughly
speaking, we obtain either all profit of non-thin items, or all profit
of thin items plus one half of the profit of non-thin items:
this gives a
3/2+\varepsilon
approximation. A further refinement of this
approach yields a
4/3+\varepsilon
approximation in the cardinality case.
We remark that, while resource augmentation is a well-established
notion in approximation algorithms, resource contraction seems to
be a rather novel direction to explore.

Theorem 71
.

For any constant
\varepsilon>0
,
there exists a polynomial-time
\frac{3}{2}+\varepsilon
approximation algorithm
for 2DGKR. In the cardinality case the approximation factor can be
improved to
\frac{4}{3}+\varepsilon
.

First, let us introduce some notations.
Without loss of generality, assume
0pt(i)\geq 0pt(i)
for all rectangles
R_{i}\in\mathcal{R}
. Now consider packing of some optimal solution in the knapsack.
Denote the strips of width
N
and height
\varepsilon N
at the top and bottom of the knapsack by
S_{T,\varepsilon}:=[0,N]\times[(1-\varepsilon)N,N]
and

S_{B,\varepsilon}:=[0,N]\times[0,\varepsilon N]
. Similarly, denote the strips of height
N
and width
\varepsilon N
to the left and right of the bin
by
S_{L,\varepsilon}:=[0,\varepsilon N]\times[0,N]
and
S_{R,\varepsilon}:=[(1-\varepsilon)N,N]\times[0,N]
.
The set of rectangles intersected by one of these strips
S_{K,\varepsilon},K\in\{T,B,L,R\}
is denoted by

E_{K,\varepsilon}
.
There are two kinds of rectangles that intersect these strips.
The set of rectangles completely contained inside one of these strips
S_{K,\varepsilon}
, is denoted by

C_{K,\varepsilon}
.
The set of remaining rectangles that do not lie completely inside the strip
S_{K,\varepsilon}
but intersect the strip, is denoted by
D_{K,\varepsilon}
(see Figure
5.3
).
With slight abuse of notations, we will assume
C_{K,\varepsilon}
also represents the embedding of corresponding rectangles in the strip.

5.1
Weighted Case

In this section we give a polynomial time
(3/2+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for the weighted 2-dimensional geometric knapsack problem when items are allowed to be rotated by
90^{\circ}
.
In contrary to the unweighted case, where it is possible to remove a constant number of
large
items, the same is not possible in the weighted case, where an item could have a big profit.

We call an item
i

massive
if
0pt(i)\geq(1-\varepsilon)N
and
0pt(i)\geq(1-\varepsilon)N
. The presence of such a big item in the optimal solution requires a different analysis, that we present below. In both the cases, we can show that there exists a container packing with roughly 2/3 of the profit of the optimal solution.

Let us assume that
\varepsilon<1/6
. We will prove the following result:

Theorem 72
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
\mathcal{R}
be a set of items that can be packed into the
N\times N
knapsack. Then there exists an
\varepsilon
-granular container packing with
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers of a subset
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
into the
N\times N
knapsack such that
p(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})\geq(2/3-O(\varepsilon))p(\mathcal{R})
, if rotations are allowed.

We start by analyzing the case of a set
\mathcal{R}
that has a massive item.

Lemma 73
.

Suppose that a set
\mathcal{R}
of items can be packed into a
N\times N
bin and there is a massive item
m\in\mathcal{R}
. Then, there is a container packing with at most
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers for a subset
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
such that
p(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})\geq\left(\dfrac{2}{3}-O(\varepsilon)\right)p(\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

Assume, without loss of generality, that
1/(3\varepsilon)
is an integer. Consider the items in
\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\}
. Clearly, each of them has width or height at most
\varepsilon
; moreover,
a(\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\})\leq(1-(1-\varepsilon)^{2})N^{2}=(2\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2})N^{2}\leq\frac{N^{2}}{2(1+\varepsilon)}
, as
\varepsilon<1/6
; thus, by possibly rotating each element so that the height is smaller than
\varepsilon
, by Theorem
3
all the items in
\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\}
can be packed in a
N\times\frac{N}{1+\varepsilon}
bin; then, by Lemma
14
, there is a container packing for a subset of
\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\}
with
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers that fits in the
N\times N
bin and has profit at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))p(\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\})

Consider now the packing of
\mathcal{R}
. Clearly, the region
[\varepsilon N,(1-\varepsilon)N]^{2}
is entirely contained within the boundaries of the massive item
m
. Partition the region with
x
-coordinate between
\varepsilon N
and
(1-\varepsilon)N
in
k=1/(3\varepsilon)
strips of width
3\varepsilon(1-2\varepsilon)N\geq 2\varepsilon N
and height
N
, let them be
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
; let
\mathcal{R}(S_{i})
be the set of items in
\mathcal{R}
such that their left or right edge (or both) are contained in the interior of strip
S_{i}
. Since each item belongs to at most two of these sets, there exists
i
such that
p(\mathcal{R}(S_{i}))\leq 6\varepsilon p(\mathcal{R})
.

Symmetrically, we define
k
horizontal strips
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
, obtaining an index
j
such that
p(\mathcal{R}(T_{j}))\leq 6\varepsilon p(\mathcal{R})
. Thus, no item in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}:=\mathcal{R}\setminus(R(S_{i})\cup R(T_{j}))
has a side contained in the interior of
S_{i}
or
T_{j}
, and
p(\overline{\mathcal{R}})\geq(1-12\varepsilon)p(\mathcal{R})
. Let
M_{V}
be the set of items in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\setminus\{m\}
that overlap
T_{j}
, and let
M_{H}
be the set of items in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\setminus\{m\}
that overlap
S_{i}
. Clearly, the items in
M_{H}
can be packed in a horizontal container with width
N
and height
N-0pt(m)
, and the items in
M_{V}
can be packed in a vertical container of width
N-0pt(m)
and height
N
.

Let
H
be the set of items of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\setminus M_{H}
that are completely above the massive item
m
or completely below it; symmetrically, let
V
be the set of items of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}\setminus M_{V}
that are completely to the left or completely to the right of
m
. We will now show that there is a container packing for
M_{H}\cup V\cup\{m\}
. Since all the elements overlapping
T_{j}
have been removed,
V
can be packed in a bin of size
(N-0pt(m))\times(1-2\varepsilon)N
(see Figure
5.1
). Since
(1-2\varepsilon)N\cdot(1+\varepsilon)<(1-\varepsilon)N\leq 0pt(m)
, Lemma
14
implies that there is a container packing of a subset of
V
with profit at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))p(V)
in a bin of size
(N-0pt(m))\times 0pt(m)
and using
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers; thus, by adding a horizontal container of the same size as
m
and a horizontal container of size
N\times(N-0pt(m))
, we obtain a container packing for
M_{H}\cup V\cup\{m\}
with
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers and profit at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))p(M_{H}\cup V\cup\{m\})
. Symmetrically, there is a container packing for a subset of
M_{V}\cup H\cup\{m\}
with profit at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))p(M_{V}\cup H\cup\{m\})
and
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{MAX}
be the set of maximum profit among the sets
\mathcal{R}\setminus\{m\}
,
M_{H}\cup V\cup\{m\}
and
M_{V}\cup H\cup\{m\}
. By the discussion above, there is a container packing for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{MAX}
with
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
containers and profit at least
(1-O(\varepsilon))p(\mathcal{R}_{MAX})
. Since each element in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is contained in at least two of the above three sets, it follows that:

\displaystyle p(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq(1-O(\varepsilon))p(\mathcal{R}_{MAX})\geq\left(1-O(\varepsilon)\right)\left(\frac{2}{3}p(\overline{\mathcal{R}})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{2}{3}-O(\varepsilon)\right)p(\mathcal{R})

∎

If there is no massive item, we will show existence of two
container packings

and show the maximum of them always packs items with total profit at least
\left(\frac{2}{3}-O(\varepsilon)\right)
fraction of the optimal profit.

First, we follow the corridor decomposition and the classification of items as in Section
4.3
to define
sets
LF,SF,LT,ST,OPT_{small}
.
Let
T:=LT\cup ST
be the set of thin items.
Also let
APX
be the best
container packing
and
OPT
be the optimal solution.
Then similar to Lemma
64
, we can show
p(APX)\geq(1-\varepsilon)(p(LF)+p(SF)+p(OPT_{small}))
.
Thus,

p(APX)\geq(1-\varepsilon)p(OPT)-p(T).

(5.1)

In the second case, we define the set
T
as above.
Then in Resource Contraction Lemma (Lemma
74
), we will show that one can pack
1/2
of the remaining profit in the optimal solution, i.e.,
p(OPT\setminus T)/2
in a
knapsack of size
N\times(1-\varepsilon/2)N
.
Now, we can pack
T
in a horizontal container of height
\varepsilon/4
and using Lemma
74
and resource augmentation we can pack
p(OPT\setminus T)/2
in the remaining space
N\times(1-\varepsilon/4)N
.
Thus,

p(APX)\geq p(T)+(1-\varepsilon)(p(OPT)-p(T))/2.

(5.2)

Hence, up to
(1-O(\varepsilon))
factor, we obtain a packing with profit at least
\max\{(p(T)+p(OPT\setminus T)/2),p(OPT\setminus T)\}\geq 2/3\cdot p(OPT)
, thus proving Theorem
72
.

Note that since Theorem
12
gives a PTAS for container packings, Theorem
71
immediately implies a
(2/3-O(\varepsilon))
-approximation algorithm.

Now to complete the proof of Theorem
71
, it only remains to prove Lemma
74
.

Lemma 74
.

(Resource Contraction Lemma)

If a set of items
M
contains no massive item and can be packed into a
N\times N
bin, then it is possible to pack a set
M^{\prime}
of profit at least
p(M)\cdot\frac{1}{2}
into a
N\times(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})N
bin (or a
(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})N\times N
bin), if rotations are allowed.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{s}=\varepsilon/2
.
We will partition
M
into two sets
M_{1},M\setminus M_{1}
and show that both these sets can be packed into
N\times(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
bin.
If an item
i
is embedded in position
(x_{i},y_{i})
, we define
x^{\prime}_{i}:=x_{i}+0pt(i),y^{\prime}_{i}:=y_{i}+0pt(i)
.

In a packing of a set of items
M
, for item
i
we define

Left(i):=\{k\in M:x^{\prime}_{k}\leq x_{i}\}
,

Right(i):=\{k\in M:x_{k}\geq x^{\prime}_{i}\}
,

Top(i):=\{k\in M:y_{k}\geq y^{\prime}_{i}\}
,

Bottom(i):=\{k\in M:y^{\prime}_{k}\leq y_{i}\}
,
i.e., the set of items that lie completely on left, right, top and bottom of
i
respectively.
Now consider four strips
S_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}},S_{B,\varepsilon_{s}},S_{L,\varepsilon_{s}},S_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
(see Figure
5.2
).

3\varepsilon_{s}N
\varepsilon_{s}N

Case 1.

Now rotate
C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
and

and pack on top of

and width

\leq(1-3\varepsilon_{s}+2\varepsilon_{s})N\leq(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
.

Case 2.

D_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\cap D_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\neq\emptyset
, i.e., there is some item intersecting
S_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
that also crosses
S_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}
. Now, there are three subcases:

Case 2A.

There exists an item
i
that does neither intersect
S_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
nor
S_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
.
Then item
i
partitions the items in
M\setminus(C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup\{i\})

into two sets:
Left(i)
and
Right(i)
.
Without loss of generality, assume
x_{i}\leq 1/2
. Then remove
Right(i),i,C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}
and
C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
from the packing.
Now rotate
C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}
and
C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
to pack right of
Left(i)
.
Define this set
M\setminus(Right(i)\cup\{i\})
to be
M_{1}
. Clearly packing of
M_{1}
takes height
N
and width
x_{i}+4\varepsilon_{s}N\leq(\frac{1}{2}+4\varepsilon_{s})N\leq(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
as
\varepsilon_{s}\leq\frac{1}{10}
.
As the item
i
does not intersect the strip
S_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
,
(Right(i)\cup\{i\})
can be packed into height
N
and width
(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
.

Case 2B.

There exists an item
i
that intersects both
S_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
and
S_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
.
Consider
M_{1}
to be
M\setminus(C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup Top(i))
.
As there is no massive item,
M_{1}
is packed in height
(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
and width
N
.
Now, pack
Top(i)
and then rotate
C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
and
C_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
to pack on top of it.
These items can be packed into height
(1-y^{\prime}_{i}+2\varepsilon_{s})N\leq 5\varepsilon_{s}N\leq(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
as
\varepsilon_{s}\leq 1/10
.

Case 2C.

If an item
i
intersects both
S_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
and

, then the item

intersects exactly one of
S_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
and
S_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
.

Consider the set of items in

.

First, consider the case when the set
D_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\cap D_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}
contains an item

(similarly one can consider
i\in D_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
). Now if
x^{\prime}_{i}\leq N/2
, take
M_{1}:=Right(i)
.
Then, we can rotate
Right(i)
and pack into height
(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
and width
N
. On the other hand,
pack
M\setminus\{M_{1}\cup C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\}

as it is. Then rotate
C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
and pack on its side. Total width
\leq(1/2+3\varepsilon_{s}+\varepsilon_{s})N\leq(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
as
\varepsilon_{s}\leq 1/6
. Otherwise if
x^{\prime}_{i}>N/2
take
M_{1}:=Left(i)\cup i
.
Now, consider packing of
M\setminus\{M_{1}\cup C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\}
, rotate
C_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
and pack on its left. Total width
\leq(1/2+4\varepsilon_{s})N\leq(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
as
\varepsilon_{s}\leq 1/10
.

Otherwise, no items in
S_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}\cap S_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}
are in
D_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup D_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
.
So let us assume that
i\in C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
(similarly one can consider
i\in C_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
), then we take
M_{1}=E_{T,3\varepsilon_{s}}\setminus(C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup C_{R,\varepsilon_{s}})
. Then we can rotate
C_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}
and
C_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}
and pack them on top of

as in Case 1.
∎

5.2
Unweighted case

In this section we present our polynomial time
(4/3+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for 2DGKR for the cardinality case.

Figure 5.3

Definitions for cardinality 2DK with rotations.

As in the case without rotations, we will first show the existence of a container packing that packs items with a total profit of
(3/4-O(\varepsilon))|OPT|
where
OPT
is the optimal solution.
Let
APX
be the largest profit solution with container packing.
As in Section
4.3
, we assume all items to be skewed. Note that small items can be handled with the techniques used in Lemma
59
.
We start with the corridor partition as in Section
4.3
and define
thin
,
fat
and
killed

rectangles accordingly.
Let
T
and
F
be the set of thin and fat rectangles respectively.

We will show that

|APX|\geq(3/4-O(\varepsilon))|OPT|
.

Lemma 75
.

|APX|\geq(1-\varepsilon)|F|
.

Proof.

After removal of
T
, we can get a container based packing for almost all items in
F
as discussed in Lemma
54
in Section
4.3
.
∎

Now the main technical contribution of this section is the following
Resource Contraction Lemma
.

Lemma 76
.

(Resource Contraction Lemma)
Suppose that there exists a feasible packing of a set of rectangles
M
in a
N\times N
bin, for some
\varepsilon>0
. Then it is possible to pack a subset of
M
with cardinality at least
\frac{2}{3}(1-O(\varepsilon))|M|
into a
N\times\left(1-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1}\right)N
bin (or a
\left(1-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1}\right)N\times N
bin), if rotations are allowed.

We defer the proof of the lemma to the end of this section. First, we show that using Lemma
76
we can prove the following:

Lemma 77
.

|APX|\geq|T|+(2/3-\varepsilon)|F|
.

Proof.

First, we use Lemma
50
such that the total height of all rectangles in
T
is at most
\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1}N}{2}
.
So we pack them in a horizontal container of height
\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1}N}{2}
. Then using Lemma
76
we show the existence of a packing of
(2/3-O(\varepsilon))|F|
in
N\times(1-\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1})N
. Then we can use resource augmentation to get a container packing of
(2/3-O(\varepsilon))|F|
in the remaining area
N\times\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+1}}{2}\right)N
.
∎

Thus we get the following theorem:

Theorem 78
.

|APX|\geq(3/4-O(\varepsilon))|OPT|
.

Proof.

The claim follows by combining Lemma
77
and
75
. Up to a factor
1-O(\varepsilon)
, the worst case is obtained when
|F|=|T|+2/3\cdot|F|
,
i.e.,
|F|=3|T|
. This gives a total profit of
3/4\cdot|T\cup F|
.
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
76
.
Let
M
be a set of rectangles that can be packed into a
N\times N
bin.
First, we show that
we can remove a set of rectangles with cardinality at most
\varepsilon|M|
such that the remaining rectangles will either be very tall (so they intersect both
S_{T,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
and
S_{B,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
) or not so tall (so that they intersect only one of
S_{T,\varepsilon^{i}}
or
S_{B,\varepsilon^{i}}
) for some
i\in[1/2\varepsilon]
.

Lemma 79
.

Given any constant
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a value
i\in[1/2\varepsilon]
such that all rectangles having height
\in((1-2\varepsilon^{i})N,(1-\varepsilon^{i+1})N]
have total profit at most
\varepsilon|M|
.

Proof.

Let
K_{i}
be the set of rectangles with height
\in((1-2\varepsilon^{i})N,(1-\varepsilon^{i+1})N]

for
i\in[1/2\varepsilon]
.
Clearly a rectangle can belong to at most two such sets. Thus, cheapest set among these
1/2\varepsilon

sets has cardinality at most
\varepsilon|M|
.
∎

Lemma 80
.

Given any constant
\varepsilon_{s}\geq\varepsilon_{small}
,
either
a(E_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup E_{R,\varepsilon_{s}})\leq\frac{(1+8\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}

or
a(E_{T,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup E_{B,\varepsilon_{s}})\leq\frac{(1+8\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}
.

Proof.

Let us define
H:=E_{L,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup E_{R,\varepsilon_{s}}

and
V:=E_{T,\varepsilon_{s}}\cup E_{B,\varepsilon_{s}}
.
Note that,

a(V)+a(H)\leq a(V\cup H)+a(V\cap H)
.
Now,
a(V\cup H)\leq N^{2}
as all rectangles were packed into a
N\times N
knapsack.
On the other hand, except possibly four rectangles (the ones that contain at least one of the points
(\varepsilon_{s}N,\varepsilon_{s}N),((1-\varepsilon_{s})N,\varepsilon_{s}N),(\varepsilon_{s}N,(1-\varepsilon_{s})N),((1-\varepsilon_{s})N,(1-\varepsilon_{s})N)
) all other rectangles in
(V\cap H)
lie entirely within the four
\varepsilon_{s}N
strips.
Thus
a(V\cap H)\leq 4\varepsilon_{s}N^{2}+4\varepsilon_{small}N^{2}\leq 8\varepsilon_{s}N^{2}
, as
\varepsilon_{small}\leq\varepsilon_{s}
.
Thus,
\min\{a(V),a(H)\}\leq\frac{a(V\cup H)+a(V\cap H)}{2}\leq\frac{(1+8\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}
.
∎

\varepsilon^{i+1}
\varepsilon^{i}

(a)

N-w(X)
2|X|/3
C_{T,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
rotated
C_{B,\varepsilon^{i+1}}

(b)

and

. Figure (a): original packing in
N\times N
, Figure (b): modified packing leaving space for resource contraction on the right.

Lemma 81
.

Given a constant
0<\varepsilon_{a}<1/2
and a set of rectangles
M:=\{1,\ldots,k\}
with
0pt(i)\geq\varepsilon_{large}N,0pt(i)\leq\varepsilon_{small}N
for all
i\in M
, if
a(M)\leq(1/2+\varepsilon_{a})N^{2}
, then
a subset of
M
with cardinality
(1-2\varepsilon_{s}-2\varepsilon_{a})|M|
can be packed into a
N\times(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
knapsack.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume the rectangles in
M
are given in nondecreasing order according to their area.
Note that
a(i)\leq\varepsilon_{s}N^{2}
for any
i\in M
.
Let,
S:=\{1,\ldots,j\}
such that
\frac{(1-2\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}a(i)\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}a(i)>\frac{(1-\varepsilon_{s})}{2}N^{2}
.
Then from Theorem
3
,
S
can be packed into
N\times(1-\varepsilon_{s})N
bin.
As we considered rectangles in the order of nondecreasing area,
\frac{|S|}{|M|}\geq\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{s}\right)}{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{a}\right)}
.
Thus,
|S|\geq\left(1-\frac{(\varepsilon_{a}+\varepsilon_{s})}{\left(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{a}\right)}\right)|M|\geq(1-2\varepsilon_{a}-2\varepsilon_{s})|M|
.
∎

From Lemma
79
, let
V^{\prime}
be the set of rectangles having height
\in[(1-2\varepsilon^{i})N,(1-\varepsilon^{i+1})N]
such that
|V^{\prime}|\leq\varepsilon|M|
.
We remove the rectangles in
V^{\prime}
.
Now consider strips
S_{L,\varepsilon^{i}},S_{R,\varepsilon^{i}},S_{T,\varepsilon^{i}},S_{B,\varepsilon^{i}}
.
From Lemma
47
, we can always choose
\varepsilon_{small}
such that
\varepsilon^{1/2\varepsilon}>\varepsilon_{small}
.
Then as
\varepsilon^{i}\geq\varepsilon_{small}
, from Lemma
80
, without loss of generality assume
that
a(E_{T,\varepsilon^{i}}\cup E_{B,\varepsilon^{i}})\leq\frac{(1+8\varepsilon^{i})}{2}a(M)
.
Let
X
be the set of rectangles that intersect both
S_{T,\varepsilon^{i}}
and
S_{B,\varepsilon^{i}}
and
Y:=\{E_{T,\varepsilon^{i}}\cup E_{B,\varepsilon^{i}}\}\setminus X
.
Define
Z:=M\setminus\{X\cup Y\cup V\}
to be the rest of the rectangles.
Let us define
w(X)=\sum_{i\in X}0pt(i)
. Now there are two cases.

Case A.

w(X)\geq 12\varepsilon^{i+1}N
.
From Lemma
79
, all rectangles in
X

intersects both
S_{T,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
and
S_{B,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
.
So, removal of all rectangles in
X,C_{T,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
and
C_{B,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
creates many empty strips of height
N
and total width of
w(X)
.
Now if in a packing, there are
k
strips of height (or width)
N
and width (or height)
w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{k}
, then one can push the rectangles in the packing to the left or right and create an empty strip of width
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}
.
Thus we can push all the remaining rectangles in
Y\cup Z

to the left so that they fit into a strip of width
N-w(X)
. Then we rotate
C_{T,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
and
C_{B,\varepsilon^{i+1}}
and pack them in two strips, each
of width
\varepsilon^{i+1}N
.
Note that
0pt(i)\leq\varepsilon^{i+1}N
for all
i\in X
.
Now take rectangles in
X
by nondecreasing width, till total width is in
[w(X)-4\varepsilon^{i+1}N,w(X)-3\varepsilon^{i+1}N]
and pack them in the remaining area (see Figure
5.4
). The cardinality of this set is at least
\frac{(w(X)-4\varepsilon^{i+1}N)}{w(X)}|X|\geq\frac{2}{3}|X|
.
Hence, at least
\frac{2}{3}|X|+|Y|+|Z|\geq\frac{2}{3}(|X|+|Y|+|Z|)
rectangles are packed into
N\times(1-\varepsilon^{i+1})N
.

\varepsilon^{i+1}
\varepsilon^{i}

(a)

rotated

(b)

:

and
